Arithmetic volume of Shtukas and Langlands duality
Zeyu Wang, Wenqing Wei

Abstract
. We extend the work of Feng–Yun–Zhang relating the arithmetic volume of Shtukas with derivatives of zeta functions by allowing arbitrary coweights for split semisimple algebraic groups. As in their original work, the formula involves some numbers called eigenweights. We obtain uniform formulas for the eigenweights in terms of the Langlands dual group, marking the first structural role for the dual group in such formulas governing derivatives of L L -functions.



1.
Introduction

1.1.
Motivation

Shimura varieties play a fundamental role in the Langlands correspondence over number fields, and their geometry encodes deep arithmetic information. One manifestation of this philosophy is that the volume of Shimura varieties is related to Dirichlet
L
-functions of number fields, while their arithmetic volume, namely the volume of integral models, is related to the first derivative of these
L
-functions. See
[
3
, §1.2]
for a summary of the literature.

The moduli of Shtukas provide the function field analogue of (integral models of) Shimura varieties. In
[
3
]
, the arithmetic volume of moduli stacks of Shtukas with minuscule modification type was studied and related to higher derivatives of the zeta function of the curve. Compared with the number field setting, two striking new features arise. First, derivatives of arbitrary order naturally appear, in contrast to the number field case where only the first derivative is expected. Second, certain nontrivial constants, referred to as
eigenweights
, enter the formulas. While their meaning remains somewhat mysterious in the number field setting, they admit a natural geometric interpretation in the function field context. This additional structure can in turn be used to predict conjectural formulas for arithmetic volumes of Shimura varieties, providing part of the motivation for the work of
[
3
]
.

However, the results of
[
3
]
are restricted to minuscule modification types, which obscures the conceptual unity of the theory. Moreover, although eigenweights admit a geometric definition, their explicit computation remains intricate. Even for groups of type
A
, the formulas obtained in
[
4
]
involve complicated information such as the character tables of symmetric groups.

In this article, we extend these results to arbitrary modification types and provide a conceptual and uniform description of eigenweights in terms of the Langlands dual group. To the best of our knowledge, this is the first instance in which the Langlands dual group plays a direct and structural role in formulas governing derivatives of
L
-functions. In addition, we carry out explicit computations of eigenweights in most cases of fundamental importance, leading to formulas that are more elementary than those previously known.

1.2.
Main result

We now present the main result of this article and recall the necessary background.

1.2.1.
Moduli stack of Shtukas

Fix a proper smooth geometrically connected curve
C
defined over a finite field
\mathbb{F}_{q}
. Let
G
be a split semisimple algebraic group defined over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
, and let
\Bun_{G}
be the moduli stack of principal
G
-bundles on
C
. Fix a maximal torus
T\subset G
. For each dominant coweight
\lambda\in X_{*}(T)_{+}
, one has the Hecke stack

Here, we use
\overline{\overleftarrow{h}}
(resp.
\overline{\overrightarrow{h}}
) to denote the map remembering only the
G
-bundle on the left (resp. right). We put an overline to distinguish it from the Hecke map that remembers also the point on the curve.

The
moduli stack of Shtukas with one leg

\Sht_{G,\leq\lambda}
is defined via the Cartesian square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sht_{G,\leq\lambda}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Hk_{G,\leq\lambda}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Bun_{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Bun_{G}\times\Bun_{G}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{\Sht}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\overline{\overleftarrow{h}},\Frob\circ\overline{\overrightarrow{h}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{\Bun_{G}}}

where
\Frob:\Bun_{G}\to\Bun_{G}
is the Frobenius map and
\Delta_{\Bun_{G}}:\Bun_{G}\to\Bun_{G}\times\Bun_{G}
is the diagonal map.

Moreover, for a sequence of dominant coweights
\lambda_{I}=(\lambda_{1},\cdots,\lambda_{r})\in X_{*}(T)^{r}_{+}
where
I=\{1,\cdots,r\}
, one can define the iterated Hecke stack

\Hk_{G,\leq\lambda_{I}}:=\Hk_{G,\leq\lambda_{1}}\times_{\Bun_{G}}\Hk_{G,\leq\lambda_{2}}\times_{\Bun_{G}}\cdots\times_{\Bun_{G}}\Hk_{G,\leq\lambda_{r}}.

1.2.2.
Arithmetic volume of the moduli of Shtukas

Let
d=\dim\Sht_{G,\leq\lambda_{I}}
. For a top-degree cohomology class
\alpha\in H^{2d}(\Sht_{G,\leq\lambda_{I}})
, viewed as a “top form,” it is natural to consider the corresponding “volume” of
\Sht_{G,\leq\lambda_{I}}
. However, this notion is not well-behaved, since
\Sht_{G,\leq\lambda_{I}}
is neither proper nor smooth. In fact, one has
H^{2d}(\Sht_{G,\leq\lambda_{I}})=0
whenever
r>0
.

In
[
3
]
, the authors introduced an
ad hoc
definition of this volume in the special case where
\alpha
can be thought of as
f_{\Sht,I}^{*}\alpha_{0}
for some
\alpha_{0}\in H^{2d}(\Hk_{G,\leq\lambda_{I}})
. The idea originates from the Grothendieck–Lefschetz trace formula. More precisely, one considers the maps

H^{*}_{c}(\Bun_{G})\xrightarrow{(\overline{\overrightarrow{h}}_{I})^{*}}IH_{c}^{*}(\Hk_{G,\leq\lambda_{I}})\xrightarrow{(\overline{\overleftarrow{h}}_{I})_{!}}H^{*-2d}_{c}(\Bun_{G}),

where
IH_{c}^{*}(\Hk_{G,\leq\lambda_{I}})
denotes the compactly supported intersection cohomology of
\Hk_{G,\leq\lambda_{I}}
.
1
1

1

In
[
3
]
, only the case where
\Hk_{G,\leq\lambda_{I}}
is smooth is considered. The extension to the non-smooth case via intersection cohomology is straightforward.
We refer to §
4.1
for a precise definition of these maps.

Given the data above, one considers the operator

(1.1)

\Gamma_{c,\alpha_{0}}:H^{*}_{c}(\Bun_{G})\to H^{*}_{c}(\Bun_{G})

defined by

\Gamma_{c,\alpha_{0}}(-):=(\overline{\overleftarrow{h}}_{I})_{!}((\overline{\overrightarrow{h}}_{I})^{*}(-)\cup\alpha_{0}).

Note that the operator
\Gamma_{c,\alpha_{0}}
preserves the cohomological degree. One defines the
arithmetic volume
of the moduli space
\Sht_{G,\leq\lambda_{I}}
with respect to “
\alpha
” to be the number

(1.2)

\mathrm{vol}(\Sht_{G,\leq\lambda_{I}},\alpha):=\operatorname{tr}(\Frob\circ\Gamma_{c,\alpha_{0}},H^{*}_{c}(\Bun_{G})).

See Remark
2.10
for a discussion of convergence issues related to this definition.

Example 1.1
.

When
r=0
, one has
\Hk_{G,\varnothing}=\Bun_{G}
and
\Sht_{G,\varnothing}^{0}=\Bun_{G}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
. We choose
\alpha_{0}=1\in H^{0}(\Bun_{G})
. In this case, we have
\mathrm{vol}(\Bun_{G}(\mathbb{F}_{q}),1)=\operatorname{tr}(\Frob,H_{c}^{*}(\Bun_{G}))
. Modulo convergence issues, the Grothendieck–Lefschetz fixed point formula predicts that
\mathrm{vol}(\Bun_{G}(\mathbb{F}_{q}),1)=|\Bun_{G}(\mathbb{F}_{q})|
. This matches the natural expectation that the volume of the discrete stack
\Bun_{G}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
is its size.

1.2.3.
Main result: same modification type

Now we specialize to a very canonical choice of
\alpha_{0}
. When
r=1
, there is a distinguished line bundle
\mathcal{L}_{\det}\in\Pic(\Hk_{G})_{\mathbb{Q}}
called the
determinant line bundle
, obtained by pulling back its local counterpart defined in §
4.1
.

For general
r
, consider the natural projections
r_{i}:\Hk_{G,\leq\lambda_{I}}\to\Hk_{G,\leq\lambda_{i}}
for
i\in I
. Define

\mathcal{L}_{\det,I}:=\bigl((r_{i})^{*}\mathcal{L}_{\det}\bigr)_{i\in I}\in\Pic(\Hk_{G,\leq\lambda_{I}})_{\mathbb{Q}}^{I}.

We take

\alpha_{0}=\prod_{i=1}^{r}c_{1}\bigl((r_{i})^{*}\mathcal{L}_{\det}\bigr)^{d_{i}}\in H^{2d}(\Hk^{\mathrm{l}}_{G,\leq\lambda_{I}}),\quad\text{where }d_{i}=\langle 2\rho,\lambda_{i}\rangle+1.

In this case, we write

(1.3)

\Gamma_{c,\mathcal{L}_{\det,I}}=\Gamma_{c,\alpha_{0}}

\mathrm{vol}(\Sht_{G,\leq\lambda_{I}},f_{\Sht,I}^{*}\mathcal{L}_{\det,I})=\mathrm{vol}(\Sht_{G,\leq\lambda_{I}},\alpha).

We first state a version of the main result when all
\{\lambda_{i},1\leq i\leq r\}
are the same, which already reflects the most interesting aspects of this problem. See §
1.2.4
for a more general version.

Theorem 1.2
.

For
\lambda_{I}=(\lambda,\lambda,\cdots,\lambda)
where
\lambda\in X_{*}(T)_{+}
, assuming
\Sht_{G,\lambda_{I}}\neq\varnothing
, we have

(1.4)

\mathrm{vol}(\Sht_{G,\leq\lambda_{I}},f_{\Sht,I}^{*}\mathcal{L}_{\det,I})=|\pi_{1}(G)|q^{\dim\Bun_{G}}(\log q)^{-r}\Bigl(\dfrac{d}{ds}\Bigr)^{r}\Big|_{s=0}\Bigl(q^{(2g-2)b_{\lambda}s}\displaystyle\prod_{i=1}^{n}\zeta_{C}(-\epsilon_{\lambda,i}s+d_{i})\Bigr).

Here,

•

n=\mathrm{rk}(G)
is the rank of the group
G
,

•

d_{i},1\leq i\leq n
are the degrees of fundamental invariants of
G
, that is, the degree of generators of the free polynomial ring
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}[\mathfrak{g}]^{G}
where
\mathfrak{g}
is the Lie algebra of
G
.

•

b_{\lambda}\in\mathbb{Q},\epsilon_{\lambda,i}\in\overline{\mathbb{Q}}
are some constants,

•

\zeta_{C}(s)
is the zeta function of
C
.

•

g
is the genus of the curve
C
.

When
\lambda
is minuscule, Theorem
1.2
is a special case of
[
3
, Theorem 1.3.8]
.
2
2

2

Strictly speaking, the line bundle used in
[
3
, Theorem 1.3.8]
is different, but it is easy to compare the two results.

The numbers
\epsilon_{\lambda,i}
are called
eigenweights
in
[
3
]
. They are rational numbers when
G
is not of type
D_{n}
for
n\geq 4
even. See Example
2.8
for the precise meaning of these numbers. In §
5.1
, we explicitly calculate these numbers for all coweights of classical groups and most fundamental coweights for exceptional groups, resulting in formulas that are more elementary than those in
[
4
]
.

The constants
b_{\lambda}
are less emphasized in
[
3
]
as they are easy when
\lambda
is minuscule. See §
5.2
for some computational results on these numbers.

In §
4
, we will give a uniform description of the constants
b_{\lambda},\epsilon_{\lambda,i}
in terms of the Langlands dual group
\check{G}
of
G
.

Remark 1.3
.

Generalization of Theorem
1.2
to the case that
G
is split reductive should be straightforward. We only state the result for semisimple groups to keep the formula clean and compact.

Remark 1.4
.

Theorem
1.2
can be regarded as an instance of relative Langlands duality in the sense of
[
1
]
: On the automorphic side, one takes the trivial spherical
G
-variety
G\curvearrowright\mathrm{point}
corresponding to the constant period; on the spectral side, one takes the twisted cotangent bundle
\check{G}\curvearrowright T^{*}_{\psi}(\check{G}/\check{N})
corresponding to the Whittaker period. From this point of view, the constants
b_{\lambda},\epsilon_{\lambda,i}
can be read off from the Poisson structure on the local Plancherel algebra introduced in
[
1
, §8]
(see Remark
4.5
).

1.2.4.
Main result: different modification types

Now we give a more general version of Theorem
1.2
in the case where
\lambda_{I}=(\lambda_{1},\cdots,\lambda_{r})\in X_{*}(T)^{r}_{+}
and the
\lambda_{i}
are not necessarily equal.

For each
i\in I
, define a differential operator on
\mathbb{R}^{n}=\{(s_{1},\cdots,s_{n})\mid s_{j}\in\mathbb{R}\}
by

(1.5)

D_{\lambda_{i}}:=(2g-2)b_{\lambda_{i}}-(\log q)^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\epsilon_{\lambda_{i},j}\partial_{s_{j}}.

Here the numbers
b_{\lambda_{i}}
and
\epsilon_{\lambda_{i},j}
are the same as those in Theorem
1.2
for the coweight
\lambda_{i}
. See §
2.4.2
for the ordering of these numbers.

Consider the
L
-function

(1.6)

\mathscr{L}_{C,G}(s_{1},\cdots,s_{n}):=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\zeta_{C}(s_{i}+d_{i}).

The following theorem is a special case of Theorem
2.13
.

Theorem 1.5
.

Under Assumption
2.12
(which always holds when
G
is not of type
D_{n}
for
n
even) and assuming
\Sht_{G,\leq\lambda_{I}}\neq\varnothing
, we have

(1.7)

\mathrm{vol}(\Sht_{G,\leq\lambda_{I}},f_{\Sht,I}^{*}\mathcal{L}_{\det,I})=|\pi_{1}(G)|q^{\dim\Bun_{G}}\big((\prod_{i=1}^{r}D_{\lambda_{i}})\mathscr{L}_{C,G}(s_{1},\cdots,s_{n})\big)\big|_{s_{1}=\cdots=s_{n}=0}.

1.3.
New ingredients

Theorem
1.5
generalizes
[
3
, Theorem 1.3.8]
beyond the minuscule case. As is clear from the formulation, the main task in proving the theorem is to compute the operator
\Gamma_{c,\mathcal{L}_{\det,I}}
in (
1.3
), which we call
(global) relative Hecke operators
. In
[
3
]
, the computation of relative Hecke operators relies on the Vinberg semigroup and the wonderful compactification of the adjoint group, which are only applicable in the minuscule case.

Instead, we develop a new approach to compute relative Hecke operators: we study their local counterparts (
2.13
), which we call
local relative Hecke operators
, and then compute the global ones via a local–global compatibility result (Theorem
3.2
). This approach has several advantages. First, it allows us to handle the non-minuscule case, and indeed all modification types in a uniform way. Second, our local models are directly related to the Satake category, which enables a uniform description of the eigenweights in terms of the Langlands dual group (achieved in §
4
). Finally, our approach is closely connected to the relative Langlands duality in the sense of
[
1
]
, and is directly comparable with the approach in
[
7
]
which deals with the strongly tempered case. This perspective suggests a unified conceptual framework for understanding Gross–Zagier type formulas over function fields, namely, formulas in which higher derivatives of
L
-functions arise. See Remark
4.5
for further discussion of this connection.

1.4.
Notations

We use
C
to denote a smooth geometrically connected projective curve defined over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
. Let
\xi\in H^{2}(C)
be the fundamental class of
C
. Let
D=\Spec\mathbb{F}_{q}[\![t]\!]
be the formal disc, and
\Aut(D)
be the group of origin fixing automorphisms of
D
.

Throughout the paper, we work with a split semisimple connected algebraic group
G
. Fix a maximal torus and Borel subgroup
T\subset B\subset G
. We use
W
to denote the Weyl group of
G
.

We use
L^{+}G,LG
to denote the jet, loop group of
G
. We use
\Gr_{G}:=LG/L^{+}G
to denote the affine Grassmannian, which is an ind-scheme. We define the local Hecke stack
\Hk_{G}^{\mathrm{l}}:=(L^{+}G\backslash\Gr_{G})/\Aut(D)
. As a global counterpart, we have the global Hecke stack
\Hk_{G}
which admits a map
\overrightarrow{h}:\Hk_{G}\to\Bun_{G}\times C
with each fiber isomorphic to
\Gr_{G}
.

For each
\lambda\in X_{*}(T)
, we have the closed Schubert cells
\Gr_{G,\leq\lambda}\subset\Gr_{G}
,
\Hk_{G,\leq\lambda}^{\mathrm{l}}\subset\Hk_{G}^{\mathrm{l}}
,
\Hk_{G,\leq\lambda}\subset\Hk_{G}
defined such that
\Gr_{G,\leq\lambda}=\Gr_{G,\leq\lambda^{+}}
where
\lambda^{+}\in X_{*}(T)_{+}
is the unique dominant element in
W\cdot\lambda\subset X_{*}(T)
.

Define
k=\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}
. For a prestack
X
. we use
\mathrm{Shv}(X)
to denote the category of (ind-)constructible étale sheaves on
X
with coefficient in
k
. We refer to
[
7
, §4.1]
for a detailed definition of this category. We use
\underline{k}_{X}\in\mathrm{Shv}(X)
to denote the constant sheaf on
X
, and
\omega_{X}=(X\to*)^{!}k\in\mathrm{Shv}(X)
to denote the dualizing sheaf on
X
whenever the
!
-pullback functor is defined. For a prestack
X
, we have its cochain complex
\Gamma(X):=\Gamma_{\textup{\'{e}t}}(X_{\overline{\mathbb{F}_{q}}},\underline{k})
. Define the cohomology ring
H^{*}(X):=\bigoplus_{d\in\mathbb{Z}}H^{i}\Gamma(X)
. We use
\langle n\rangle=[n](n/2)
to denote the Tate twist by
n/2
and cohomological shift by
n
.

Although this is not essential for our purposes, we work by default with
(\infty,1)
-categories. For a category
\mathcal{C}
and objects
x,y\in\mathcal{C}
, we denote by
\Hom(x,y)
the mapping space from
x
to
y
, and write
\Hom^{0}(x,y):=\pi_{0}\big(\Hom(x,y)\big)
.

2.
General formalism

In this section, we set up a general framework for defining and computing the operator in (
1.3
), which we call
(global) relative Hecke operators
. Namely, whenever the operator has a local origin (coming from a local volume datum as defined in Definition
2.1
), we are able to compute it. The main result of this section is Theorem
2.13
, which generalizes Theorem
1.2
and Theorem
1.5
.

We refer to §
1.4
for basic notations.

2.1.
Volume data

We first introduce the general setup that we can define and compute the relative Hecke operators. To summarize, whenever one has some local volume data (to be introduced in §
2.1.1
), we will be able to define some relative Hecke operators (to be defined in §
2.2.1
).

2.1.1.
Local volume data

The local Hecke stack
\Hk_{G}^{\mathrm{l}}
introduced in §
1.4
fits into a correspondence

(2.1)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{B}(L^{+}G\rtimes\Aut(D))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Hk_{G}^{\mathrm{l}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{B}(L^{+}G\rtimes\Aut(D))}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overrightarrow{h}_{\mathrm{l}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overleftarrow{h}_{\mathrm{l}}}

where the two maps have clear meaning from the description
\Hk_{G}^{\mathrm{l}}=(L^{+}G\rtimes\Aut(D))\backslash(LG\rtimes\Aut(D))/(L^{+}G\rtimes\Aut(D))
.

Definition 2.1
.

We define a
local volume datum
to be a triple
v=(\mathcal{K},\mathfrak{c}^{\mathrm{l}},\mathfrak{d}^{\mathrm{l}})
in which:

•

\mathcal{K}\in\mathrm{Shv}(\Hk_{G}^{\mathrm{l}})
is a sheaf supported on
\Hk_{G,\leq\lambda}^{\mathrm{l}}\subset\Hk_{G}^{\mathrm{l}}
for some
\lambda\in X_{*}(T)_{+}
.

•

\mathfrak{c}^{\mathrm{l}}\in\mathrm{Corr}_{\Hk_{G}^{\mathrm{l}},\mathcal{K}\langle-d_{\mathfrak{c}}\rangle}(\underline{k}_{\mathbb{B}(L^{+}G\rtimes\Aut(D))},\underline{k}_{\mathbb{B}(L^{+}G\rtimes\Aut(D))})
is a
cohomological correspondence
.
3
3

3

We refer to
[
7
, §4.2]
for a general treatment of cohomological correspondences with kernels. Here, one can forget about the interpretation as a cohomological correspondence and directly work with the Hom space.
Here,

(2.2)

\mathrm{Corr}_{\Hk_{G}^{\mathrm{l}},\mathcal{K}\langle-d_{\mathfrak{c}}\rangle}(\underline{k}_{\mathbb{B}(L^{+}G\rtimes\Aut(D))},\underline{k}_{\mathbb{B}(L^{+}G\rtimes\Aut(D))})=\Hom^{0}(\overleftarrow{h}_{\mathrm{l},!}\mathcal{K}\langle-d_{\mathfrak{c}}\rangle,\underline{k}_{\mathbb{B}(L^{+}G\rtimes\Aut(D))}).

•

\mathfrak{d}^{\mathrm{l}}\in\Hom^{0}(\underline{k}_{\mathbb{B}(L^{+}G\rtimes\Aut(D))},\overrightarrow{h}_{\mathrm{l},!}\mathcal{K}\langle d_{\mathfrak{d}}\rangle)
.
4
4

4

The element
\mathfrak{d}^{\mathrm{l}}
can be regarded as a cohomological correspondence with kernel the Verdier dual of
\mathcal{K}
.

Here,
d_{\mathfrak{c}},d_{\mathfrak{d}}\in\mathbb{Z}
are some arbitrary integers, which are part of the datum. Define the
degree
of the local volume datum to be the integer
d_{v}=d_{\mathfrak{c}}+d_{\mathfrak{d}}
.

Example 2.2

(Local volume datum for determinant line bundle)
.

For each dominant coweight
\lambda\in X_{*}(T)_{+}
, we have the local volume datum
v_{\lambda,\det}=(\IC_{\lambda},\mathfrak{c}^{\mathrm{l}}_{\lambda,\det},\mathfrak{d}^{\mathrm{l}}_{\lambda})
(see Definition
4.1
).

Example 2.3

(Cup product with a cohomology class)
.

For each local volume datum
v=(\mathcal{K},\mathfrak{c}^{\mathrm{l}},\mathfrak{d}^{\mathrm{l}})
and a cohomology class
\alpha\in H^{|\alpha|}(\Hk_{G}^{\mathrm{l}})
, one can define a new local volume datum
\alpha\cdot v:=(\mathcal{K},\alpha\cup\mathfrak{c}^{\mathrm{l}},\mathfrak{d}^{\mathrm{l}})
which has degree
d_{v}+|\alpha|
.

2.1.2.
Global volume data

We now introduce global volume data.

Definition 2.4
.

We define a
global volume datum
to be a triple
v=(\mathcal{K},\mathfrak{c},\mathfrak{d})
in which:

•

\mathcal{K}\in\mathrm{Shv}(\Hk_{G})
is a sheaf supported on
\Hk_{G,\leq\lambda}\subset\Hk_{G}
for some
\lambda\in X_{*}(T)_{+}
.

•

\mathfrak{c}\in\mathrm{Corr}_{\Hk_{G},\mathcal{K}\langle-d_{\mathfrak{c}}\rangle}(\underline{k}_{\Bun_{G}\times C},\underline{k}_{\Bun_{G}\times C})
is a cohomological correspondence. Here,

(2.3)

\mathrm{Corr}_{\Hk_{G},\mathcal{K}\langle-d_{\mathfrak{c}}\rangle}(\underline{k}_{\Bun_{G}\times C},\underline{k}_{\Bun_{G}\times C})=\Hom^{0}(\overleftarrow{h}_{!}\mathcal{K}\langle-d_{\mathfrak{c}}\rangle,\underline{k}_{\Bun_{G}\times C}).

•

\mathfrak{d}\in\Hom^{0}(\underline{k}_{\Bun_{G}\times C},\overrightarrow{h}_{!}\mathcal{K}\langle d_{\mathfrak{d}}\rangle)
.

Here,
d_{\mathfrak{c}},d_{\mathfrak{d}}\in\mathbb{Z}
are some arbitrary integers, which are part of the datum. Define the degree of the global volume datum to be the integer
d_{v}=d_{\mathfrak{c}}+d_{\mathfrak{d}}
. Here, the maps
\overleftarrow{h},\overrightarrow{h}
are the top maps in (
2.4
).

2.1.3.
Local-to-global construction

From a local volume datum
(\mathcal{K},\mathfrak{c}^{\mathrm{l}},\mathfrak{d}^{\mathrm{l}})
, we can construct a global volume datum
f^{*}v=(f_{\Hk}^{*}\mathcal{K},\mathfrak{c},\mathfrak{d})
as follows: Consider the diagram

(2.4)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Bun_{G}\times C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Hk_{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Bun_{G}\times C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{B}(L^{+}G\rtimes\Aut(D))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Hk_{G}^{\mathrm{l}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{B}(L^{+}G\rtimes\Aut(D))}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overrightarrow{h}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overleftarrow{h}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{\Hk}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overrightarrow{h}_{\mathrm{l}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overleftarrow{h}_{\mathrm{l}}}

in which the vertical maps are given by restriction to the formal disc near the point on the curve
C
. The top horizontal row is the global counterpart of the correspondence (
2.1
). Both squares in (
2.4
) are Cartesian.

Define

(2.5)

\mathfrak{c}:=f^{*}\mathfrak{c}^{\mathrm{l}}\in\mathrm{Corr}_{\Hk_{G},f_{\Hk}^{*}\mathcal{K}\langle-d_{\mathfrak{c}}\rangle}(\underline{k}_{\Bun_{G}\times C},\underline{k}_{\Bun_{G}\times C})=\Hom^{0}(\overleftarrow{h}_{!}f_{\Hk}^{*}\mathcal{K}\langle-d_{\mathfrak{c}}\rangle,\underline{k}_{\Bun_{G}\times C})

as the composition

\overleftarrow{h}_{!}f_{\Hk}^{*}\mathcal{K}\langle-d_{\mathfrak{c}}\rangle\cong f^{*}\overleftarrow{h}_{\mathrm{l},!}\mathcal{K}\langle-d_{\mathfrak{c}}\rangle\xrightarrow{f^{*}\mathfrak{c}^{\mathrm{l}}}f^{*}\underline{k}_{\mathbb{B}(L^{+}G\rtimes\Aut(D))}\cong\underline{k}_{\Bun_{G}\times C},

and

(2.6)

\mathfrak{d}:=f^{*}\mathfrak{d}^{\mathrm{l}}\in\Hom^{0}(\underline{k}_{\Bun_{G}\times C},\overrightarrow{h}_{!}f_{\Hk}^{*}\mathcal{K}\langle d_{\mathfrak{d}}\rangle)

as the composition

\underline{k}_{\Bun_{G}\times C}\cong f^{*}\underline{k}_{\mathbb{B}(L^{+}G\rtimes\Aut(D))}\xrightarrow{f^{*}\mathfrak{d}^{\mathrm{l}}}f^{*}\overrightarrow{h}_{\mathrm{l},!}\langle d_{\mathfrak{d}}\rangle\cong\overrightarrow{h}_{!}f_{\Hk}^{*}\mathcal{K}\langle d_{\mathfrak{d}}\rangle.

Throughout the article, we will always work with global volume data coming from local volume data as above.

2.2.
Relative Hecke operators

In this section, we will introduce relative Hecke operators associated with volume data in both local and global settings.

•

In §
2.2.1
, we introduce global relative Hecke operators, which will exactly generalize the operators in (
1.3
).

•

In §
2.2.2
, we introduce local relative Hecke operators, which serve as local models for the global ones.

2.2.1.
Global relative Hecke operators

Now we introduce global relative Hecke operators associated to a global volume datum
v=(\mathcal{K},\mathfrak{c},\mathfrak{d})
.

We have a map

(2.7)

\Gamma_{c,v}^{C}:H^{*}_{c}(\Bun_{G}\times C)\xrightarrow{H_{c}^{*}(\mathfrak{d})}H^{*+d_{\mathfrak{d}}}_{c}(\Hk_{G},\mathcal{K})\xrightarrow{H_{c}^{*}(\mathfrak{c})}H^{*+d_{v}}_{c}(\Bun_{G}\times C).

Since the (graded) dual of
H_{c}^{*}(\Bun_{G}\times C)
is
H^{*}(\Bun_{G}\times C)(\dim\Bun_{G}+1)
. Ignoring the Tate twist, the (graded) dual of (
2.7
) is

(2.8)

\Gamma_{v}^{C}:H^{*}(\Bun_{G}\times C)\xrightarrow{H^{*}(\mathfrak{c})}H_{-(*+d_{\mathfrak{c}})}^{BM}(\Hk_{G}/\Bun_{G}\times C,\mathcal{K})\xrightarrow{H^{*}(\mathfrak{d})}H^{*+d_{v}}(\Bun_{G}\times C)

in which
H_{-*}^{BM}(\Hk_{G}/\Bun_{G}\times C,\mathcal{K})=\Hom^{*}(\mathcal{K},\omega_{\Hk_{G}/\Bun_{G}\times C})
. Here, we are using

\omega_{\Hk_{G}/\Bun_{G}\times C}=\overleftarrow{h}^{!}\underline{k}_{\Bun_{G}\times C}\cong\overrightarrow{h}^{!}\underline{k}_{\Bun_{G}\times C}.

The maps in (
2.8
) are defined by
H^{*}(\mathfrak{c})=H_{c}^{*}(\mathfrak{c})^{*}
,
H^{*}(\mathfrak{d})=H_{c}^{*}(\mathfrak{d})^{*}
. More explicitly, the first map can be identified with the map

\begin{split}H^{*}(\mathfrak{c}):H^{*}(\Bun_{G}\times C)&=\Hom^{*}(\underline{k}_{\Bun_{G}\times C},\underline{k}_{\Bun_{G}\times C})\\
&\to\Hom^{*}(\overleftarrow{h}_{!}\mathcal{K}\langle-d_{\mathfrak{c}}\rangle,\underline{k}_{\Bun_{G}\times C})\\
&\cong\Hom^{*}(\mathcal{K}\langle-d_{\mathfrak{c}}\rangle,\overleftarrow{h}^{!}\underline{k}_{\Bun_{G}\times C})\\
&=H_{-(*+d_{\mathfrak{c}})}^{BM}(\Hk_{G}/\Bun_{G}\times C,\mathcal{K})\end{split}.

The second map can be identified with

\begin{split}H^{*}(\mathfrak{d}):H_{-*}^{BM}(\Hk_{G}/\Bun_{G}\times C,\mathcal{K})&\cong\Hom^{*}(\mathcal{K},\overrightarrow{h}^{!}\underline{k}_{\Bun_{G}\times C})\\
&\cong\Hom^{*}(\overrightarrow{h}_{!}\mathcal{K},\underline{k}_{\Bun_{G}\times C})\\
&\to\Hom^{*}(\underline{k}_{\Bun_{G}\times C}\langle-d_{\mathfrak{d}}\rangle,\underline{k}_{\Bun_{G}\times C})\\
&=H^{*+d_{\mathfrak{d}}}(\Bun_{G}\times C)\end{split}.

The maps
H^{*}(\mathfrak{c}),H^{*}(\mathfrak{d})
are linear over
H^{*}(\Bun_{G}\times C)
. That is, for any
\alpha\in H^{*}(\Bun_{G}\times C)
, we have

(2.9)

H^{*}(\mathfrak{c})(\alpha\cdot-)=(\overleftarrow{h}^{*}\alpha)\cdot-

and

(2.10)

H^{*}(\mathfrak{d})((\overrightarrow{h}^{*}\alpha)\cdot-)=\alpha\cdot-.

We further define the map

(2.11)

\Gamma_{c,v}:H^{*}_{c}(\Bun_{G})\xrightarrow{\pr_{1}^{*}}H^{*}_{c}(\Bun_{G}\times C)\xrightarrow{\Gamma_{c,v}^{C}}H^{*+d_{v}}_{c}(\Bun_{G}\times C)\xrightarrow{\pr_{1,!}}H^{*+d_{v}-2}_{c}(\Bun_{G})

and its dual

(2.12)

\Gamma_{v}:H^{*}(\Bun_{G})\xrightarrow{\pr_{1}^{*}}H^{*}(\Bun_{G}\times C)\xrightarrow{\Gamma_{v}^{C}}H^{*+d_{v}}(\Bun_{G}\times C)\xrightarrow{\pr_{1,!}}H^{*+d_{v}-2}(\Bun_{G}).

We call operators defined in (
2.7
)(
2.8
)(
2.11
)(
2.12
)
global relative Hecke operators
.

Example 2.5
.

When
v=f^{*}v_{\lambda,\det}
where
v_{\lambda,\det}
is in Example
2.2
, we have
\Gamma_{c,f^{*}v_{\lambda,\det}}=\Gamma_{c,\mathcal{L}_{\det,I}}
where the later is defined in (
1.3
) in which one takes
r=1
and
\lambda_{I}=(\lambda)
.

2.2.2.
Local relative Hecke operators

Now we introduce local relative Hecke operators associated to a local volume datum
v=(\mathcal{K},\mathfrak{c}^{\mathrm{l}},\mathfrak{d}^{\mathrm{l}})
as defined in Definition
2.1
. They will play an important role in the computation of global relative Hecke operators and in formulating the main theorem.

Note that (
2.8
) has a local counterpart

(2.13)

\Gamma_{v}^{\mathrm{l}}:H^{*}(\mathbb{B}(L^{+}G\rtimes\Aut(D)))\xrightarrow{H^{*}(\mathfrak{c}^{\mathrm{l}})}H_{-(*+d_{\mathfrak{c}})}^{BM}(\Hk_{G}^{\mathrm{l}}/\mathbb{B}(L^{+}G\rtimes\Aut(D)),\mathcal{K})\xrightarrow{H^{*}(\mathfrak{d}^{\mathrm{l}})}H^{*+d_{v}}(\mathbb{B}(L^{+}G\rtimes\Aut(D))).

Here, the maps
H^{*}(\mathfrak{c}^{\mathrm{l}})
and
H^{*}(\mathfrak{d}^{\mathrm{l}})
are linear over
H^{*}(\mathbb{B}(L^{+}G\rtimes\Aut(D)))
. We call operators
\Gamma_{v}^{\mathrm{l}}
defined in (
2.13
)
local relative Hecke operators
.

2.3.
Calculating local relative Hecke operators

Now we study local relative Hecke operators in more detail.

2.3.1.
Cohomology of
\mathbb{B}(L^{+}G\rtimes\Aut(D))

Let
R=H^{*}(\mathbb{B}T)
. Then
H^{*}(\mathbb{B}G)=R^{W}
and
H^{*}(\mathbb{B}(L^{+}G\rtimes\Aut(D)))=R^{W}[\hbar]
, where
\hbar:=c_{1}(T_{D})\in H^{2}([D/\Aut(D)])\cong H^{2}(\mathbb{B}\Aut(D))
. Here
T_{D}
is the tangent bundle of
D
.

Note that
H^{*}(\mathbb{B}G)
has a natural augmentation ideal
H^{>0}(\mathbb{B}G)\subset H^{*}(\mathbb{B}G)
. This defines a decreasing
augmentation filtration

\{F_{\mathrm{aug}}^{\blacktriangleright}H^{*}(\mathbb{B}G)\}_{\blacktriangleright\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}
on
H^{*}(\mathbb{B}G)
such that

F_{\mathrm{aug}}^{i}H^{*}(\mathbb{B}G):=(H^{>0}(\mathbb{B}G))^{i}\subset H^{*}(\mathbb{B}G).

Define the
Gross motive

(2.14)

\mathbb{V}:=\Gr_{\mathrm{aug}}^{1}H^{*}(\mathbb{B}G).

We have a canonical isomorphism
\Gr^{\blacktriangleright}_{\mathrm{aug}}(H^{*}(\mathbb{B}G))\cong\Sym^{\blacktriangleright}(\mathbb{V})
.

2.3.2.
Local relative Hecke operator on associated graded

We keep working with a local volume datum
v=(\mathcal{K},\mathfrak{c}^{\mathrm{l}},\mathfrak{d}^{\mathrm{l}})
and assume
d_{v}=2
.

Consider maps

\overleftarrow{h}^{*}_{\mathrm{l}},\overrightarrow{h}_{\mathrm{l}}^{*}:H^{*}(\mathbb{B}(L^{+}G\rtimes\Aut(D)))\to H^{*}(\Hk_{G}^{\mathrm{l}})

where
\overleftarrow{h}_{\mathrm{l}},\overrightarrow{h}_{\mathrm{l}}
are the bottom maps in (
2.4
). For
\alpha\in H^{|\alpha|}(\mathbb{B}(L^{+}G\rtimes\Aut(D)))
, define

(2.15)

[\alpha]=\hbar^{-1}\cdot(\overleftarrow{h}_{\mathrm{l}}^{*}\alpha-\overrightarrow{h}^{*}_{\mathrm{l}}\alpha)\in H^{|\alpha|-2}(\Hk_{G}^{\mathrm{l}}).

Here, we are using the fact that
H^{*}(\Hk_{G}^{\mathrm{l}})
is a flat
k[\hbar]
-module.

Considering

(2.16)

\widetilde{\nabla}_{v}^{\mathrm{l}}:=\Gamma_{[-]\cdot v}^{\mathrm{l}}(1)\in\End(H^{*}(\mathbb{B}(L^{+}G\rtimes\Aut(D))))

where the dot product in
[-]\cdot v
is understood as in Example
2.3
. Since
d_{v}=2
, we know
\Gamma_{v}^{\mathrm{l}}(1)\in H^{2}(\mathbb{B}(L^{+}G\rtimes\Aut(D)))=k\cdot\hbar
. Write

(2.17)

\Gamma_{v}^{\mathrm{l}}(1)=-b_{v}\hbar

for
b_{v}\in k
.

For any
\alpha\in H^{*}(\mathbb{B}(L^{+}G\rtimes\Aut(D)))
, note that

(2.18)

\begin{split}\Gamma_{v}^{\mathrm{l}}(\alpha)&=H^{*}(\mathfrak{d}^{\mathrm{l}})\circ H^{*}(\mathfrak{c}^{\mathrm{l}})(\alpha)\\
&=H^{*}(\mathfrak{d}^{\mathrm{l}})((\overleftarrow{h}_{\mathrm{l}})^{*}(\alpha)H^{*}(\mathfrak{c}^{\mathrm{l}})1)\\
&=H^{*}(\mathfrak{d}^{\mathrm{l}})((\overrightarrow{h}_{\mathrm{l}})^{*}(\alpha)+\hbar[\alpha]H^{*}(\mathfrak{c}^{\mathrm{l}})1)\\
&=\alpha\Gamma_{v}^{\mathrm{l}}(1)+\hbar\cdot\Gamma_{[\alpha]\cdot v}^{\mathrm{l}}(1)\\
&=-b_{v}\hbar\cdot\alpha+\hbar\cdot\widetilde{\nabla}_{v}^{\mathrm{l}}(\alpha)\end{split}.

Define
\nabla_{v}^{\mathrm{l}}:=\widetilde{\nabla}_{v}^{\mathrm{l}}\otimes_{k[\hbar]}k\in\End(H^{*}(\mathbb{B}G))
. We have the following important observation:

Lemma 2.6
.

The map
\nabla_{v}^{\mathrm{l}}:H^{*}(\mathbb{B}G)\to H^{*}(\mathbb{B}G)
is a derivation. Moreover, it preserves the natural augmentation filtration
F_{\mathrm{aug}}^{\blacktriangleright}H^{*}(\mathbb{B}G)
defined in §
2.3.1
.

Proof.

Both follow from the computation that

(2.19)

\widetilde{\nabla}_{v}^{\mathrm{l}}(\alpha\beta)=\alpha\widetilde{\nabla}_{v}^{\mathrm{l}}(\beta)+\widetilde{\nabla}_{v}^{\mathrm{l}}(\alpha)\beta+\hbar\Gamma_{[\alpha][\beta]\cdot v}^{\mathrm{l}}(1)

for any
\alpha,\beta\in H^{*}(\mathbb{B}(L^{+}G\rtimes\Aut(D)))
.
∎

Given Lemma
2.6
, it is natural to consider

(2.20)

E_{v}:=\Gr^{1}_{\mathrm{aug}}\nabla_{v}^{\mathrm{l}}:\mathbb{V}\to\mathbb{V}.

We would like to call this the
relative Hecke operator on Gross motive
. Its eigenvalues form a (multi-)set

(2.21)

\{\epsilon_{v,i}\mid 1\leq i\leq\dim\mathbb{V}\}

and are called
eigenweights
of
v
. See §
2.4.2
for an ordering of the eigenweights when
G
is almost simple.

We have the following proposition to be compared with its global counterpart, Proposition
3.7
:

Proposition 2.7
.

The induced map
\overline{\Gamma}_{v}^{\mathrm{l}}:=(\hbar^{-1}\Gamma_{v}^{\mathrm{l}})\otimes_{k[\hbar]}k
preserves the filtration
F_{\mathrm{aug}}^{\blacktriangleright}H^{*}(\mathbb{B}G)
. Under the canonical isomorphism
H^{*}(\mathbb{B}G)\cong\Sym^{\blacktriangleright}(\mathbb{V})
, we have
\Gr^{\blacktriangleright}_{\mathrm{aug}}\overline{\Gamma}_{v}^{\mathrm{l}}=-b_{v}\cdot\mathrm{id}+\nabla_{E_{v}}\in\End(\Sym^{\blacktriangleright}(\mathbb{V}))
. Here
\nabla_{E_{v}}
is the derivation on
\Sym^{\blacktriangleright}(\mathbb{V})
such that
\nabla_{E_{v}}|_{\mathbb{V}}=E_{v}
.

Example 2.8
.

When
v=v_{\lambda,\det}
as in Example
2.2
, we have
\epsilon_{\lambda,i}=\epsilon_{v_{\lambda,\det},i}
and
b_{\lambda}=b_{v_{\lambda,\det}}
. This gives the numbers involved in Theorem
1.2
.

2.4.
Volume of Shtukas: a general formulation

Now we formulate a general version of Theorem
1.5
.

2.4.1.
Definition of volume

For each local volume datum
v=(\mathcal{K},\mathfrak{c}^{\mathrm{l}},\mathfrak{d}^{\mathrm{l}})
as in Definition
2.1
and
e\in\pi_{1}(G)\cong\pi_{0}(\Bun_{G})
, assuming
\mathcal{K}\in\mathrm{Shv}(\Hk_{G}^{\mathrm{l},e_{\mathcal{K}}})
for some
e_{\mathcal{K}}\in\pi_{1}(G)
,
5
5

5

One has connected components decomposition
\Hk_{G}^{\mathrm{l}}=\coprod_{e\in\pi_{1}(G)}\Hk_{G}^{\mathrm{l},e}
we consider the corresponding global volume datum
f^{*}v
as defined in §
2.1.3
.

We have the global relative Hecke operators

(2.22)

\Gamma_{c,f^{*}v}^{e}:H^{*}_{c}(\Bun_{G}^{e})\to H^{*+d_{v}-2}_{c}(\Bun_{G}^{e+e_{\mathcal{K}}})

and

(2.23)

\Gamma_{f^{*}v}^{e}:H^{*}(\Bun_{G}^{e+e_{\mathcal{K}}})\to H^{*+d_{v}-2}(\Bun_{G}^{e})

which are given by restricting those in §
2.2.1
to one connected component.

Fix
I=\{1,2,\cdots,r\}
for some
r\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
and choose a sequence of local volume data
v_{I}:=(v_{i}=(\mathcal{K}_{i}\in\mathrm{Shv}(\Hk_{G}^{\mathrm{l},e_{i}}),\mathfrak{c}^{\mathrm{l}}_{i},\mathfrak{d}^{\mathrm{l}}_{i}))_{i\in I}
such that
d_{v_{i}}=d_{\mathfrak{c}_{i}}+d_{\mathfrak{d}_{i}}=2
for any
i\in I
. Let
e_{v_{I}}=\sum_{i}e_{i}\in\pi_{1}(G)
. Define the global relative Hecke operator attached to
v_{I}

(2.24)

\Gamma_{c,f^{*}v_{I}}^{e}:=\Gamma_{c,f^{*}v_{1}}^{e+e_{v_{I}}-e_{1}}\circ\cdots\circ\Gamma_{c,f^{*}v_{r}}^{e}:H^{*}_{c}(\Bun_{G}^{e})\to H_{c}^{*}(\Bun_{G}^{e+e_{v_{I}}})

and its dual

(2.25)

\Gamma_{f^{*}v_{I}}^{e}:=\Gamma_{f^{*}v_{r}}^{e}\circ\cdots\circ\Gamma_{f^{*}v_{1}}^{e+e_{v_{I}}-e_{1}}:H^{*}(\Bun_{G}^{e+e_{v_{I}}})\to H^{*}(\Bun_{G}^{e}).

Moreover, we make the following assumption:

Assumption 2.9
.

Each local volume datum
v_{i}
is defined over a subfield
E\subset k
such that
E/\mathbb{Q}
is a finite extension. That is, the sheaf
\mathcal{K}_{i}
is defined over
E
and the maps
\mathfrak{c}_{i}^{\mathrm{l}},\mathfrak{d}_{i}^{\mathrm{l}}
are defined over
E
.

Define

(2.26)

\mathrm{vol}(\Sht_{G,I}^{e},f^{*}v_{I}):=\begin{cases}0&,e_{v_{I}}\neq 0\\
\operatorname{tr}(\Frob\circ\Gamma_{c,f^{*}v_{I}}^{e},H_{c}^{*}(\Bun_{G}^{e}))&,e_{v_{I}}=0\end{cases}.

One can also define the volume for all connected components

(2.27)

\mathrm{vol}(\Sht_{G,I},f^{*}v_{I}):=\operatorname{tr}(\Frob\circ\Gamma_{c,v_{I}},H_{c}^{*}(\Bun_{G})).

Then one has
\mathrm{vol}(\Sht_{G,I},f^{*}v_{I})=\sum_{e\in\pi_{1}(G)}\mathrm{vol}(\Sht_{G,I}^{e},f^{*}v_{I})
.

Remark 2.10
.

The trace above should be understood as the summation

\operatorname{tr}(\Frob\circ\Gamma_{c,f^{*}v_{I}},H^{*}_{c}(\Bun_{G}))=\sum_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\operatorname{tr}(\Frob\circ\Gamma_{c,f^{*}v_{I}},H^{i}_{c}(\Bun_{G})),

in which each vector space
H^{i}_{c}(\Bun_{G})
is finite-dimensional. Under Assumption
2.9
, this sum is absolutely convergent under any identification
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}\cong\mathbb{C}
. This can be proved in the same way as
[
3
, Proposition 5.5.3]
.

Example 2.11
.

When taking
v_{I}=(v_{\lambda_{1},\det},\cdots,v_{\lambda_{r},\det})
where each term is as in Example
2.2
for
\lambda_{I}\in X_{*}(T)^{r}
. We have
\mathrm{vol}(\Sht_{G,I},f^{*}v_{I})=\mathrm{vol}(\Sht_{G,\leq\lambda_{I}},f_{\Sht,I}^{*}\mathcal{L}_{\det,I})
where the later is considered in Theorem
1.5
.

2.4.2.
Ordering the eigenweights

Recall the (multi-)set of eigenweights

\{\epsilon_{v,i}\mid 1\leq i\leq n\}
defined in (
2.21
). When
G
is almost simple, we refine this multiset by partially introducing an ordering using the decomposition
\mathbb{V}=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}(-d_{i})
, where
\{d_{i}\mid 1\leq i\leq n\}
are as in Theorem
1.2
.

When
G
is not of type
D_{n}
for
n
even, the numbers
\{d_{i},1\leq i\leq n\}
are distinct and we order them such that
d_{1}<d_{2}<\cdots<d_{n}
. Define
\mathbb{V}_{i}:=\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}(-d_{i})
, and we have
\mathbb{V}=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{V}_{i}
. We define
E_{v}|_{\mathbb{V}_{i}}=-\epsilon_{v,i}\cdot\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{V}_{i}}
. In this case, the matrix
E_{v}
is diagonal under the decomposition
\mathbb{V}=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{V}_{i}
.

When
G
is of type
D_{n}
for
n
even, we take
d_{i}=2i
for
1\leq i\leq n-1
and
d_{n}=n
. When
i\neq n/2,n
, we define
\mathbb{V}_{i}:=\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}(-d_{i})
. When
i\in\{n/2,n\}
, the degree
n
part of
\mathbb{V}
is a two dimensional vector space. We do the following instead: The outer automorphism group
S_{2}\cong\mathrm{Out}(G)
acts on
\mathbb{V}
. We define
\mathbb{V}_{n/2}
as the
S_{2}
-invariant part of the degree
n
part of
\mathbb{V}
, and define
\mathbb{V}_{n}
as the part that
S_{2}
acts via the non-trivial character.
6
6

6

The one-dimensional vector space
\mathbb{V}_{n}
is spanned by the
Pfaffian
.
We still have
\mathbb{V}=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{V}_{i}
. For
i\neq n/2,n
, we define
E_{v}|_{\mathbb{V}_{i}}=-\epsilon_{v,i}\cdot\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{V}_{i}}
. When
i\in\{n/2,n\}
, we only define the (multi-)set
\{\epsilon_{v,n/2},\epsilon_{v,n}\}
as the set of eigenvalues of the
2\times 2
-matrix
-E_{v}|_{\mathbb{V}_{n/2}\oplus\mathbb{V}_{n}}
. In this case, the matrix
E_{v}
is block diagonal under the decomposition
\mathbb{V}=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{V}_{i}
with a unique
2\times 2
-block
E_{v}|_{\mathbb{V}_{n/2}\oplus\mathbb{V}_{n}}
.

2.4.3.
Main result

Now we formulate a general version of Theorem
1.5
. Take
v_{I}
as before. We make the following assumption.

Assumption 2.12
.

We assume that
\{E_{v_{i}}\in\End(\mathbb{V})\}_{i\in I}
pairwise commute. Here
E_{v_{i}}
are defined in (
2.20
).

Note that Assumption
2.12
is an empty assumption when
G
is not of type
D_{n}
for
n
even. When
G
is of type
D_{n}
for
n
even, Assumption
2.12
amounts to requiring the
2\times 2
-matrices
E_{v_{i}}|_{\mathbb{V}_{n/2}\oplus\mathbb{V}_{n}}
to be mutually commutative. Under this assumption, we can simultaneously upper-triangularize the matrices
E_{v_{i}}|_{\mathbb{V}_{n/2}\oplus\mathbb{V}_{n}}
. We can order each set
\{\epsilon_{v_{i},n/2},\epsilon_{v_{i},n}\}
such that each number
\epsilon_{v_{i},n}
is an eigenvalue for a joint eigenvector of all
E_{v_{i}}|_{\mathbb{V}_{n/2}\oplus\mathbb{V}_{n}}
.

Define the differential operator on
\mathbb{R}^{n}

(2.28)

D_{v_{i}}=(2g-2)b_{v_{i}}-(\log q)^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\epsilon_{v_{i},j}\partial_{s_{j}}

where
b_{v_{i}}
are defined in (
2.17
).

Theorem 2.13
.

Let
v_{I}=\{v_{i}\}_{i=1,\cdots,n}
be a collection of local volume data such that
d_{v_{i}}=2
for each
i\in I
and
e_{v_{I}}=0
. Under Assumption
2.12
and Assumption
2.9
, for each
e\in\pi_{1}(G)
, we have

(2.29)

\mathrm{vol}(\Sht_{G,I}^{e},f^{*}v_{I})=q^{\dim\Bun_{G}}\big((\prod_{i=1}^{r}D_{v_{i}})\mathscr{L}_{C,G}(s_{1},\cdots,s_{n})\big)\big|_{s_{1}=\cdots=s_{n}=0}.

Under the case Example
2.11
, Theorem
2.13
becomes Theorem
1.5
.

The proof of Theorem
2.13
will be given in §
3
.

Remark 2.14
.

Assumption
2.12
can fail. See
[
4
, Remark 1.3.9]
for a counterexample. This also follows from our computation of
E_{v_{\lambda,\det}}
in the type
D
case in §
5.1.7
.

3.
Calculating global relative Hecke operators

This section is devoted to the proof of Theorem
2.13
, which will be given in §
3.4
.

3.1.
Cohomology of
\Bun_{G}

We now recall the Atiyah–Bott formula for the cohomology ring
H^{*}(\Bun_{G})
and the filtrations on it as considered in
[
3
]
.

3.1.1.
Augmentation filtration

Recall the connected component decomposition
\Bun_{G}=\coprod_{e\in\pi_{1}(G)}\Bun_{G}^{e}
where
\pi_{1}(G)
is the algebraic fundamental group of
G
. For each
e\in\pi_{1}(G)
, the cohomology ring
H^{*}(\Bun_{G})
has a natural augmentation ideal
H^{>0}(\Bun_{G}^{e})\subset H^{*}(\Bun_{G}^{e})
. We get a decreasing augmentation filtration
\{F_{\mathrm{aug}}^{\blacktriangleright}H^{*}(\Bun_{G}^{e})\}_{\blacktriangleright\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}
on
H^{*}(\Bun_{G}^{e})
such that

F_{\mathrm{aug}}^{i}H^{*}(\Bun_{G}^{e}):=(H^{>0}(\Bun_{G}^{e}))^{i}\subset H^{*}(\Bun_{G}^{e}).

3.1.2.
Atiyah–Bott formula

Consider the map

f:\Bun_{G}\times C\to\mathbb{B}(L^{+}G\rtimes\Aut(D)).

It induces a map
7
7

7

We write
H_{\bullet}(C)
instead of
H_{*}(C)
to match the homological grading here with the Ran grading in §
3.1.3
.

\taut:H^{*}(\mathbb{B}(L^{+}G\rtimes\Aut(D)))\otimes H_{\bullet}(C)\to H^{*}(\Bun_{G})

defined as

\taut(\gamma\otimes z)=\int_{z}f^{*}\gamma

where
\int_{z}:H^{i}(C)\to k
is the natural integration map and
\pr_{1}^{\Bun_{G}}:\Bun_{G}\times C\to\Bun_{G}
is the projection.

For each
e\in\pi_{1}(G)
, one can further restrict to a single connected component and get

\taut^{e}:H^{*}(\mathbb{B}(L^{+}G\rtimes\Aut(D)))\otimes H_{\bullet}(C)\to H^{*}(\Bun_{G}^{e}).

We abbreviate
\taut(\gamma\otimes z)=\gamma^{z}
. We call classes of the form
\gamma^{z}

tautological classes
.

Note that the map

\taut^{e}|_{H^{*}(\mathbb{B}G)\otimes H_{\bullet}(C)}:H^{*}(\mathbb{B}G)\otimes H_{\bullet}(C)\to H^{*}(\Bun_{G})

preserves augmentation filtrations on both sides. Here, we are considering the filtration
F_{\mathrm{aug}}^{i}(H^{*}(\mathbb{B}G)\otimes H_{\bullet}(C)):=F_{\mathrm{aug}}^{i}H^{*}(\mathbb{B}G)\otimes H_{\bullet}(C)
on the left-hand-side. Therefore, we get an induced homomorphism

\Gr^{\blacktriangleright}_{\mathrm{aug}}(\taut^{e}|_{H^{*}(\mathbb{B}G)\otimes H_{\bullet}(C)}):\Gr_{\mathrm{aug}}^{\blacktriangleright}(H^{*}(\mathbb{B}G)\otimes H_{\bullet}(C))\to\Gr_{\mathrm{aug}}^{\blacktriangleright}H^{*}(\Bun_{G}^{e}).

The Atiyah–Bott formula can be formulated as follows:

Theorem 3.1
.

The map
\Gr^{\blacktriangleright}_{\mathrm{aug}}(\taut^{e}|_{H^{*}(\mathbb{B}G)\otimes H_{\bullet}(C)})|_{\mathbb{V}\otimes H_{\bullet}(C)}:\mathbb{V}\otimes H_{\bullet}(C)\to\Gr_{\mathrm{aug}}^{\blacktriangleright}H^{*}(\Bun_{G}^{e})
induces a ring isomorphism

\mathrm{AB}^{e}:\Sym^{\blacktriangleright}(\mathbb{V}\otimes H_{\bullet}(C))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\Gr_{\mathrm{aug}}^{\blacktriangleright}H^{*}(\Bun_{G}^{e}).

Theorem
3.1
is first proved in
[
6
]
in positive characteristic. See
[
3
, Theorem 4.2.8]
for this formulation and a generalization.

3.1.3.
Ran filtration

Now we introduce the Ran grading considered in
[
3
, §5.5.4]
. For each
e\in\pi_{1}(G)
, define the
Ran filtration

\{F^{\mathrm{Ran}}_{\bullet}H^{*}(\Bun_{G}^{e})\}_{\bullet\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}
such that
F^{\mathrm{Ran}}_{i}H^{*}(\Bun_{G}^{e})\subset H^{*}(\Bun_{G}^{e})
is spanned by those elements of the form
\gamma_{1}^{z_{1}}\cdots\gamma_{k}^{z_{k}}
such that
\sum_{i}|z_{k}|\leq i
. Here, we have
\gamma_{s}\in H^{*}(\mathbb{B}(L^{+}G\rtimes\Aut(D)),z_{s}\in H_{\bullet}(C)
, and
|z_{s}|
is the homological degree of
z_{s}
, and
k
is any non-negative integer.

Note that the Ran filtration is multiplicative, therefore, the associated graded
\Gr^{\mathrm{Ran}}_{\bullet}H^{*}(\Bun_{G}^{e})
carries a natural commutative ring structure and a natural augmentation filtration
\{F_{\mathrm{aug}}^{\blacktriangleright}\Gr^{\mathrm{Ran}}_{\bullet}H^{*}(\Bun_{G}^{e})\}_{\blacktriangleright\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}
.

3.2.
Commutator relation

We first introduce the key ingredient in the proof of Theorem
2.13
: Theorem
3.2
, which is a special case of the commutator relations conjectured in
[
7
, §4.5]
.

We have a commutative diagram

(3.1)

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{88.88454pt}{-14.547pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{64}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

The map
f^{*}:R^{W}[\hbar]=H^{*}(\mathbb{B}(L^{+}G\rtimes\Aut(D)))\to H^{*}(\Bun_{G}\times C)
admits the following explicit description: Consider the map
\ev:\Bun_{G}\times C\to\mathbb{B}G
and the induced map
\ev^{*}:R^{W}=H^{*}(\mathbb{B}G)\to H^{*}(\Bun_{G}\times C)
. We have

(3.2)

f^{*}\alpha=\ev^{*}\alpha

for
\alpha\in R^{W}\subset R^{W}[\hbar]
and

(3.3)

f^{*}\hbar=1\otimes c_{1}(T_{C})=1\otimes(2-2g)\xi\in H^{*}(\Bun_{G}\times C).

Here
\xi\in H^{2}(C)
is the fundamental class of
C
.

Consider the maps

\overleftarrow{f}:\Hk_{G}\times C\xrightarrow{\overleftarrow{h}\times\mathrm{id}}\Bun_{G}\times C\times C\xrightarrow{\pr_{1,3}}\Bun_{G}\times C\xrightarrow{f}\mathbb{B}(L^{+}G\rtimes\Aut(D))

and

\overrightarrow{f}:\Hk_{G}\times C\xrightarrow{\overrightarrow{h}\times\mathrm{id}}\Bun_{G}\times C\times C\xrightarrow{\pr_{1,3}}\Bun_{G}\times C\xrightarrow{f}\mathbb{B}(L^{+}G\rtimes\Aut(D)).

Consider the map
\Delta_{\Hk}=(\mathrm{id},l):\Hk_{G}\to\Hk_{G}\times C
where
l:\Hk_{G}\to C
is the map remembering the leg. We have a Gysin homomorphism

\Delta_{\Hk,!}:H^{*}(\Hk_{G})\to H^{*+2}(\Hk_{G}\times C)

and the map
f_{\Hk}^{*}:H^{*}(\mathbb{B}(L^{+}G\rtimes\Aut(D)))\to H^{*}(\Hk_{G})
.

Theorem 3.2
.

For any
\alpha\in H^{i}(\mathbb{B}(L^{+}G\times\Aut(D)))
, we have

(3.4)

\overleftarrow{f}^{*}\alpha-\overrightarrow{f}^{*}\alpha=\Delta_{\Hk,!}f^{*}_{\Hk}[\alpha]\in H^{i}(\Hk_{G}\times C)

where
[\alpha]
is defined in (
2.15
).

The proof of Theorem
3.2
will be given in §
3.5
.

Remark 3.3
.

Theorem
3.2
has an equivalent formulation, Theorem
3.8
. The proof of these two equivalent theorems confirms
[
7
, Conjecture 4.45]
in the special case that
X=\textup{point}
and one of
V,W
is the trivial representation. Since
[
7
, Assumption 4.46]
is not satisfied in Theorem
3.8
, the method of proof in
[
7
]
is not applicable here.

Remark 3.4
.

It is obvious that both sides of (
3.4
) have the same image under the map
\Delta_{\Hk}^{*}:H^{i}(\Hk_{G}\times C)\to H^{i}(\Hk_{G})
. Indeed, we have
\Delta_{\Hk}^{*}(\overleftarrow{f}^{*}\alpha-\overrightarrow{f}^{*}\alpha)=f_{\Hk}^{*}(\overleftarrow{h}^{*}_{\mathrm{l}}\alpha-\overrightarrow{h}^{*}_{\mathrm{l}}\alpha)=f_{\Hk}^{*}(\hbar\cdot[\alpha])=c_{1}(T_{C})\cdot f_{\Hk}^{*}[\alpha]=\Delta_{\Hk}^{*}\Delta_{\Hk,!}f^{*}_{\Hk}[\alpha]
. This verifies the compatibility between the sign on both sides of (
3.4
) and the normalization
\hbar=c_{1}(T_{D})\in H^{2}(\mathbb{B}\Aut(D))
.

For later use, we note the following immediate corollary of Theorem
3.2
:

Corollary 3.5
.

For any
\alpha\in H^{i}(\mathbb{B}(L^{+}G\rtimes\Aut(D)))
and
z\in H_{*}(C)
, we have

(3.5)

\overleftarrow{h}^{*}(\pr_{1}^{\Bun_{G}})^{*}\alpha^{z}-\overrightarrow{h}^{*}(\pr_{1}^{\Bun_{G}})^{*}\alpha^{z}=f_{\Hk}^{*}[\alpha]\cup l^{*}\mathrm{PD}(z)

where
\pr_{1}^{\Bun_{G}}:\Bun_{G}\times C\to C
is the projection to the first factor and
\overleftarrow{h},\overrightarrow{h},f_{\Hk}
are the maps in (
2.4
). The map
\mathrm{PD}:H_{*}(C)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}H^{2-*}(C)
is the isomorphism induced from the Poincaré duality characterized by
\int_{\xi}(\zeta\cup\mathrm{PD}(z))=\int_{z}\zeta
for every
\zeta\in H^{*}(C)
.

Proof.

Consider the map
\pr_{1}^{\Hk_{G}}:\Hk_{G}\times C\to\Hk_{G}
(resp.
\pr_{2}^{\Hk_{G}}:\Hk_{G}\times C\to C
) given by projection to the first (resp. second) factor. Note that the Gysin map can be written as

\Delta_{\Hk,!}=\Delta_{\Hk,!}\Delta_{\Hk}^{*}(\pr_{1}^{\Hk_{G}})^{*}=(\pr_{1}^{\Hk_{G}})^{*}(-)\cup\Delta_{\Hk,!}1=\pr_{1}^{*}(-)\cup(1\otimes\xi-\beta+\xi\otimes 1)

where
\beta=\sum\zeta_{i}\otimes\zeta^{i}
for a basis
(\zeta_{i})
of
H^{1}(C)
with dual basis
(\zeta^{i})
satisfying
\zeta_{i}\cup\zeta^{j}=\delta_{ij}\xi
. The equation (
3.4
) can be rewritten as

(3.6)

\overleftarrow{f}^{*}\alpha-\overrightarrow{f}^{*}\alpha=(\pr_{1}^{\Hk_{G}})^{*}f^{*}_{\Hk}[\alpha]\cup(1\otimes\xi-\beta+\xi\otimes 1).

The desired identity follows from applying
\int_{z}=(\pr_{1}^{\Hk_{G}})_{!}(-\cup(\pr_{2}^{\Hk})^{*}\mathrm{PD}(z))
to the identity (
3.6
).

∎

3.3.
Comparing local and global relative Hecke operators

Now we apply Theorem
3.2
to compute the global relative Hecke operators
\Gamma_{v}
. We fix a local volume datum
v=(\mathcal{K},\mathfrak{c}^{\mathrm{l}},\mathfrak{d}^{\mathrm{l}})
and study the map
\Gamma_{v}^{e}
evaluated on tautological classes.

Using (
3.5
), for each
\alpha\in H^{*}(\mathbb{B}(L^{+}G\rtimes\Aut(D)))
and
z\in H_{*}(C)
, we have

(3.7)

\begin{split}\Gamma_{v}(\alpha^{z})&=(\pr_{1}^{\Bun_{G}})_{!}H^{*}(\mathfrak{d})H^{*}(\mathfrak{c})(\pr_{1}^{\Bun_{G}})^{*}\alpha^{z}\\
&=(\pr_{1}^{\Bun_{G}})_{!}H^{*}(\mathfrak{d})(\overleftarrow{h}^{*}(\pr_{1}^{\Bun_{G}})^{*}\alpha^{z}\cdot H^{*}(\mathfrak{c})1)\\
&=(\pr_{1}^{\Bun_{G}})_{!}H^{*}(\mathfrak{d})((\overrightarrow{h}^{*}(\pr_{1}^{\Bun_{G}})^{*}\alpha^{z}+f_{\Hk}^{*}[\alpha]\cup l^{*}\mathrm{PD}(z))\cdot H^{*}(\mathfrak{c})1)\\
&=(\pr_{1}^{\Bun_{G}})_{!}((\pr_{1}^{\Bun_{G}})^{*}\alpha^{z}\cdot H^{*}(\mathfrak{d})H^{*}(\mathfrak{c})1)+(\pr_{1}^{\Bun_{G}})_{!}H^{*}(\mathfrak{d})(f_{\Hk}^{*}[\alpha]\cup l^{*}\mathrm{PD}(z)\cdot H^{*}(\mathfrak{c})1)\\
&=\alpha^{z}\cdot(\pr_{1}^{\Bun_{G}})_{!}(f^{*}H^{*}(\mathfrak{d}^{\mathrm{l}})H^{*}(\mathfrak{c}^{\mathrm{l}})1)+(\pr_{1}^{\Bun_{G}})_{!}(f^{*}H^{*}(\mathfrak{d}^{\mathrm{l}})([\alpha]\cdot H^{*}(\mathfrak{c}^{\mathrm{l}})1)\cup l^{*}\mathrm{PD}(z))\\
&=\alpha^{z}\cdot\Gamma_{v}^{\mathrm{l}}(1)^{\xi}+\widetilde{\nabla}_{v}^{\mathrm{l}}(\alpha)^{z}\\
&=(2g-2)b_{v}\cdot\alpha^{z}+\widetilde{\nabla}_{v}^{\mathrm{l}}(\alpha)^{z}\end{split}

where the map
\Gamma_{v}^{\mathrm{l}}
is defined in (
2.13
) and the map
\widetilde{\nabla}_{v}^{\mathrm{l}}
is defined in (
2.16
). This is the global counterpart of (
2.18
).

Similarly, for
\alpha_{i}\in H^{*}(\mathbb{B}(L^{+}G\rtimes\Aut(D))),z_{i}\in H_{*}(C),i\in I
, we have

(3.8)

\begin{split}\Gamma_{v}(\prod_{i\in I}\alpha_{i}^{z_{i}})=\sum_{J\subset I}\pm(\prod_{j\in J}\alpha_{j}^{z_{j}})\pr_{1,!}(f^{*}H^{*}(\mathfrak{d}^{\mathrm{l}})(\prod_{i\in I\backslash J}[\alpha_{i}]\cdot H^{*}(\mathfrak{c}^{\mathrm{l}})1)\cup\prod_{i\in I\backslash J}\mathrm{PD}(z_{i}))\\
=\sum_{J\subset I}\pm(\prod_{j\in J}\alpha_{j}^{z_{j}})\Gamma_{({\prod_{i\in I\backslash J}[\alpha_{i}]})\cdot v}^{\mathrm{l}}(1)^{\cap_{i\in I\backslash J}z_{i}}.\end{split}

Here, we define
z_{i}\cap z_{j}=\mathrm{PD}^{-1}(\mathrm{PD}(z_{i})\cup\mathrm{PD}(z_{j}))
.

This formula is parallel to
[
3
, (5.5.13)]
and is a global analogue of (
2.19
).

3.4.
Global relative Hecke operators on associated graded

We have the following global analogue of Lemma
2.6
:

Lemma 3.6
.

The map
\Gamma_{v}^{e}:H^{*}(\Bun_{G}^{e+e_{\mathcal{K}}})\to H^{*}(\Bun_{G}^{e})
preserves the Ran filtration
F^{\mathrm{Ran}}_{\bullet}
defined on both sides in §
3.1.3
. Moreover, the induced map on associated graded
8
8

8

The local analogue of taking associated graded with respect to the Ran filtration should be regarded as taking
-\otimes_{k[\hbar]}k
.

\Gr^{\mathrm{Ran}}_{\bullet}\Gamma_{v}^{e}:\Gr^{\mathrm{Ran}}_{\bullet}H^{*}(\Bun_{G}^{e+e_{\mathcal{K}}})\to\Gr^{\mathrm{Ran}}_{\bullet}H^{*}(\Bun_{G}^{e})
is a derivation, which preserves the augmentation filtration
F^{\blacktriangleright}_{\mathrm{aug}}
on both sides.

Proof.

The first claim follows from
|z_{i}\cap z_{j}|\leq\min\{|z_{i}|,|z_{j}|\}
for any
z_{i},z_{j}\in H_{\bullet}(C)
. The second claim follows from the observation that only the terms in (
3.8
) satisfying
|J|\geq|I|-1
survive under
\Gr_{\bullet}^{\mathrm{Ran}}\Gamma_{v}^{e}
. The last claim, concerning the preservation of the augmentation filtration, follows from (
3.7
) and the Atiyah–Bott formula (Theorem
3.1
).
∎

By Lemma
3.6
, we get a derivation

\Gr_{\mathrm{aug}}^{\blacktriangleright}\Gr^{\mathrm{Ran}}_{\bullet}\Gamma_{v}^{e}:\Gr_{\mathrm{aug}}^{\blacktriangleright}\Gr^{\mathrm{Ran}}_{\bullet}H^{*}(\Bun_{G}^{e+e_{\mathcal{K}}})\to\Gr_{\mathrm{aug}}^{\blacktriangleright}\Gr^{\mathrm{Ran}}_{\bullet}H^{*}(\Bun_{G}^{e}).

By Theorem
3.1
, we have
\Gr_{\mathrm{aug}}^{\blacktriangleright}\Gr^{\mathrm{Ran}}_{\bullet}H^{*}(\Bun_{G}^{e+e_{\mathcal{K}}})\cong\Sym^{\blacktriangleright}(\mathbb{V}\otimes H_{\bullet}(C))
. Under this isomorphism, we can identify the map above as

(3.9)

\Gr_{\mathrm{aug}}^{\blacktriangleright}\Gr^{\mathrm{Ran}}_{\bullet}\Gamma_{v}^{e}:\Sym^{\blacktriangleright}(\mathbb{V}\otimes H_{\bullet}(C))\to\Sym^{\blacktriangleright}(\mathbb{V}\otimes H_{\bullet}(C)).

On the other hand, the local relative Hecke operator on the Gross motive (
2.20
) induces a derivation

\nabla_{E_{v}\otimes\mathrm{id}}:\Sym^{\blacktriangleright}(\mathbb{V}\otimes H_{\bullet}(C))\to\Sym^{\blacktriangleright}(\mathbb{V}\otimes H_{\bullet}(C)),

defined by requiring that its restriction to generators is given by
\nabla_{E_{v}\otimes\mathrm{id}}|_{\mathbb{V}\otimes H_{\bullet}(C)}=E_{v}\otimes\mathrm{id}
, and extended to the symmetric algebra via the Leibniz rule. We have the following key observation, which is a global counterpart of Proposition
2.7
, generalizing
[
3
, Proposition 5.6.17]
:

Proposition 3.7
.

We have
\Gr_{\mathrm{aug}}^{\blacktriangleright}\Gr^{\mathrm{Ran}}_{\bullet}\Gamma_{v}^{e}=(2g-2)b_{v}\cdot\mathrm{id}+\nabla_{E_{v}\otimes\mathrm{id}}\in\End(\Sym^{\blacktriangleright}(\mathbb{V}\otimes H_{\bullet}(C)))
.

Proof.

This is immediate from (
3.8
) and (
3.7
).
∎

Proof of Theorem
2.13
.

The proof given in
[
3
, §5.6.18]
translates verbatimly after replacing
[
3
, Proposition 5.6.17]
by Proposition
3.7
.
∎

3.5.
Proof of Theorem
3.2

In this section, we prove the key identity (
3.4
).

3.5.1.
Multiplicativity in
\alpha

We first prove that (
3.4
) holds for
\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}
if it holds for
\alpha_{1}
and
\alpha_{2}
. Indeed, assume (
3.4
) holds for
\alpha_{1}
and
\alpha_{2}
. We have

\begin{split}\overleftarrow{f}^{*}(\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2})-\overrightarrow{f}^{*}(\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2})&=\overleftarrow{f}^{*}\alpha_{1}(\overleftarrow{f}^{*}\alpha_{2}-\overrightarrow{f}^{*}\alpha_{2})+(\overleftarrow{f}^{*}\alpha_{1}-\overrightarrow{f}^{*}\alpha_{1})\overrightarrow{f}^{*}\alpha_{2}\\
&=\overleftarrow{f}^{*}\alpha_{1}\Delta_{\Hk,!}f_{\Hk}^{*}[\alpha_{2}]+\Delta_{\Hk,!}f_{\Hk}^{*}[\alpha_{1}]\overrightarrow{f}^{*}\alpha_{2}\\
&=\Delta_{\Hk,!}(\overleftarrow{h}^{*}f^{*}\alpha_{1}f_{\Hk}^{*}[\alpha_{2}]+f_{\Hk}^{*}[\alpha_{1}]\overrightarrow{h}^{*}f^{*}\alpha_{2})\\
&=\Delta_{\Hk,!}f_{\Hk}^{*}(\overleftarrow{h}_{\mathrm{l}}^{*}\alpha_{1}[\alpha_{2}]+[\alpha_{1}]\overrightarrow{h}_{\mathrm{l}}^{*}\alpha_{2})\\
&=\Delta_{\Hk,!}f_{\Hk}^{*}[\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}]\end{split}.

This verifies (
3.4
) for
\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}
.

3.5.2.
Functoriality for
G_{1}\to G_{2}

Assume we have a group homomorphism between split reductive groups
G_{1}\to G_{2}
, which induces maps
\phi_{\mathrm{l}}:\mathbb{B}(L^{+}G_{1}\rtimes\Aut(D))\to\mathbb{B}(L^{+}G_{2}\rtimes\Aut(D))
,
\phi:\Bun_{G_{1}}\to\Bun_{G_{2}}
,
\phi_{\mathrm{l},\Hk}:\Hk_{G_{1}}^{\mathrm{l}}\to\Hk_{G_{2}}^{\mathrm{l}}
, and
\phi_{\Hk}:\Hk_{G_{1}}\to\Hk_{G_{2}}
. We claim that (
3.4
) for
\alpha\in H^{*}(\mathbb{B}(L^{+}G_{2}\times\Aut(D)))
implies (
3.4
) for
\phi_{\mathrm{l}}^{*}\alpha\in H^{*}(\mathbb{B}(L^{+}G_{1}\times\Aut(D)))
. Indeed, assume (
3.4
) holds for
\alpha\in H^{*}(\mathbb{B}(L^{+}G_{2}\times\Aut(D)))
. We have

\begin{split}\overleftarrow{f}^{*}(\phi_{\mathrm{l}}^{*}\alpha)-\overrightarrow{f}^{*}(\phi_{\mathrm{l}}^{*}\alpha)&=(\phi_{\Hk}\times\mathrm{id}_{C})^{*}(\overleftarrow{f}^{*}\alpha-\overrightarrow{f}^{*}\alpha)\\
&=(\phi_{\Hk}\times\mathrm{id}_{C})^{*}\Delta_{\Hk,!}f_{\Hk}^{*}[\alpha]\\
&=\Delta_{\Hk,!}f_{\Hk}^{*}\phi_{\mathrm{l},\Hk}^{*}[\alpha]\\
&=\Delta_{\Hk,!}f_{\Hk}^{*}[\phi_{\mathrm{l}}^{*}\alpha]\end{split}.

This verifies (
3.4
) for
\phi_{\mathrm{l}}^{*}\alpha
.

3.5.3.
Equivalent formulation

Note that Theorem
3.2
is equivalent to the following:

Theorem 3.8
.

For each
\mathcal{K}\in\mathrm{Shv}(\Hk_{G}^{\mathrm{l}})^{\heartsuit}
,
m\in H^{*}(\mathcal{K})
, and
\alpha\in H^{*}(\mathbb{B}(L^{+}G\rtimes\Aut(D)))
, we have

(3.10)

\overleftarrow{f}^{*}\alpha\pr_{1}^{*}f_{\Hk}^{*}m-\overrightarrow{f}^{*}\alpha\pr_{1}^{*}f_{\Hk}^{*}m=\Delta_{\Hk,!}f^{*}_{\Hk}([\alpha]m)\in H^{*}(\Hk_{G}\times C,\pr_{1}^{*}f_{\Hk}^{*}\mathcal{K})

where
\pr_{1}:\Hk_{G}\times C\to\Hk_{G}
is the projection to the first factor and
f_{\Hk}^{*}:H^{*}(\Hk^{\mathrm{l}}_{G},\mathcal{K})\to H^{*}(\Hk_{G},f_{\Hk}^{*}\mathcal{K})
is the pullback map.

To see the equivalence, in one direction, since
\Delta_{\Hk,!}f^{*}_{\Hk}([\alpha]m)=\Delta_{\Hk,!}f^{*}_{\Hk}([\alpha])\cdot\pr_{1}^{*}f_{\Hk}^{*}m
, we know that (
3.10
) can be obtained from the identity (
3.4
) by acting on
\pr_{1}^{*}f_{\Hk}^{*}m
. Therefore, Theorem
3.2
implies Theorem
3.8
.

Conversely, assume Theorem
3.8
is true. To see (
3.4
), we only need to show that any element
\alpha\in H^{*}(\Hk_{G}\times C)
annihilating
\pr_{1}^{*}f_{\Hk}^{*}m\in I\!H^{*}(\Hk_{G,\leq\lambda}\times C)
for all
\lambda\in X_{*}(T)_{+}
and
m\in I\!H^{*}(\Hk_{G,\leq\lambda}^{\mathrm{l}})
is zero. Since

H^{*}(\Hk_{G}\times C)=\mathrm{lim}_{\lambda}H^{*}(\Hk_{G,\leq\lambda}\times C),

we only need to show that for any
\lambda\in X_{*}(T)_{+}
, any element
\alpha\in H^{*}(\Hk_{G,\leq\lambda}\times C)
annihilating

\pr_{1}^{*}f_{\Hk}^{*}I\!H^{*}(\Hk_{G,\leq\lambda}^{\mathrm{l}})\subset I\!H^{*}(\Hk_{G,\leq\lambda}\times C)

is zero. By Künneth formula, we are reduced to show that the annihilator of

f_{\Hk}^{*}I\!H^{*}(\Hk_{G,\leq\lambda}^{\mathrm{l}})\subset I\!H^{*}(\Hk_{G,\leq\lambda})

in
H^{*}(\Hk_{G,\leq\lambda})
is zero. Note that we have

H^{*}(\Hk_{G,\leq\lambda})=H^{*}(\Bun_{G}\times C)\otimes_{R^{W}[\hbar]}H^{*}(\Hk_{G,\leq\lambda}^{\mathrm{l}})

and

I\!H^{*}(\Hk_{G,\leq\lambda})=H^{*}(\Bun_{G}\times C)\otimes_{R^{W}[\hbar]}I\!H^{*}(\Hk_{G,\leq\lambda}^{\mathrm{l}}).

Since the map
H^{*}(\Hk_{G,\leq\lambda}^{\mathrm{l}})\to\End_{R^{W}[\hbar]}(I\!H^{*}(\Hk_{G,\leq\lambda}^{\mathrm{l}}))
is injective. We only need to show that

Q:=\coker(H^{*}(\Hk_{G,\leq\lambda}^{\mathrm{l}})\to\End_{R^{W}[\hbar]}(I\!H^{*}(\Hk_{G,\leq\lambda}^{\mathrm{l}})))

is a free
R^{W}[\hbar]
-module. By
[
5
, Theorem 1.5]
, we know

[H^{*}(\Hk_{G,\leq\lambda}^{\mathrm{l}})\to\End_{R^{W}[\hbar]}(I\!H^{*}(\Hk_{G,\leq\lambda}^{\mathrm{l}}))]\otimes_{R^{W}[\hbar]}k=[H^{*}(\Gr_{G,\leq\lambda})\to\End(I\!H^{*}(\Gr_{G,\leq\lambda}))]

is an injection. Since both
\End_{R^{W}[\hbar]}(I\!H^{*}(\Hk_{G,\leq\lambda}^{\mathrm{l}}))
and
H^{*}(\Hk_{G,\leq\lambda}^{\mathrm{l}})
are free
R^{W}[\hbar]
-modules, we know that
\mathrm{Tor}^{\leq-1}_{R^{W}[\hbar]}(Q,k)=0
. Since
Q
is a finitely generated graded
R^{W}[\hbar]
-module, this implies that
Q
is a free
R^{W}[\hbar]
-module.

3.5.4.
Propagation through convolution

We claim that Theorem
3.8
is stable under the convolution of
\mathrm{Shv}(\Hk_{G}^{\mathrm{l}})^{\heartsuit}
, that is, assuming (
3.10
) holds for
m_{1}\in H^{*}(\mathcal{K}_{1})
and
m_{2}\in H^{*}(\mathcal{K}_{2})
for
\mathcal{K}_{1},\mathcal{K}_{2}\in\mathrm{Shv}(\Hk_{G}^{\mathrm{l}})^{\heartsuit}
, one can deduce that (
3.10
) holds for
m_{1}\otimes m_{2}\in H^{*}(\mathcal{K}_{1}*\mathcal{K}_{2})\cong H^{*}(\mathcal{K}_{1})\otimes_{R^{W}[\hbar]}H^{*}(\mathcal{K}_{2})
.

Consider the global iterated Hecke stack
\widetilde{\Hk}_{G}
defined by the Cartesian diagram

\hbox to127pt{\vbox to53.07pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 63.49803pt\lower-24.9028pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-14.78429pt}{-14.8903pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{64}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
